  Correct me if I'm wrong, but I think that in your "When not to use "no" with any, many, much, or enough", the example to explain "no" with enough is incorrect. 
  You used "I do not have enough money" "I do no have enough money", but that is actually not showing when not to use no with enough, but when not to use no with a verb. I would say a correct example is "It is not enough" "It is no enough". 
  Otherwise, if you use the I do not have enough money you're only explaining when to use no or not with a verb.
  Please tell me if what I'm saying is incorrect, I would like to know.

  I hope one day I will have an opportunity to work with you!
  PS Who should we put the article AN before the word opportunity if the very noun here is uncountable? Why do we have to put articles before uncountable articles?
    ↳ **@kathymichael7224** (👍0 · 2020-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=if-Ck-cxm-w&lc=UgyaKr4Vo-DnUcmYICd4AaABAg.8Gcc0owHlXA95ypuKkhKIe
      AN is used before singular nouns that begin with a vowel. For instance we say "an egg" -- "I do not have an egg." But we say "a book"-- "I read a book" because "book" starts with a consonant.

    ↳ **@rodrigojose915** (👍0 · 2020-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=if-Ck-cxm-w&lc=Ugwg8eoKzu6o9XGtG0B4AaABAg.8GbWqBLqqJF95gIkJTpfTb
      Both of the sentences are correct. I am no angel and I am not an angel When we use no in a sentence like the one you mentioned: I am no angel it means that we want to emphasize what we're saying. So, in that case I am no angel I want to say that in no way I'm an angel, there's no how for me to be an angel, I don't want to be or I don't want to accept that fact. In this case is used when something is a fact. If someone come to you and state you're an angel (because it's a fact) you use NO to simply not accept this fact. Usually is used like this. Hope it helped. (Of course nobody is no angel. When I said It's a fact I just used the sentence I'm no angel as an example to explain the use of NO.

  @famas2007 This is another slang term that we Americans use. "What have you" is kind of like saying "etc." or "whatever you had" or "whatever you wish." 
  
  In this case he said, "You were here from January to June, or what have you." The "what have you" here just means, "or however long you were here." I hope that makes sense.

**@DemiBroadHorizonsEnglish** (👍26 · 2017-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D3u40rnqgF8&lc=UghDfu-Vk7O4aHgCoAEC
  Another tip would be to keep yourself constantly immersed in the language by making English a part of your daily routine. Take an online course for your academic studies in English, pursue some professional development in English (i.e. attend international conventions, seminars, etc. on your field but in the English language), or take up a hobby like writing short stories or blogging in English. There are also so many ways to entertain yourself in English: watch a TV series, documentaries, or stand-up comedies with and then without English subtitles, look up the meaning of the lyrics of your favourite songs, and these are just to name a few. This approach will help you learn a great deal over time and you'll get better at English without even realising it. As for the speaking practice, you could arrange some on-site or online speaking sessions with an English teacher, keep in touch with any English-speaking friends or relatives abroad, find some pen-friends and language buddies, or even attend a summer school at some point. Hope this helps! Keep working hard everyone! You can do it. :)

**@Deborah-j5w** (👍1 · 2024-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-SqpAsNBE5A&lc=Ugxop1yv3dtxY11A2s14AaABAg
  Active sentences are characterized by the subject performing the action expressed by the verb, resulting in clearer and stronger writing. Passive sentences reverse the structure, positioning the object before the verb, leading to weaker and less direct expressions. Here are some examples demonstrating the distinction between active and passive voice sentences:
  
  Active Voice:
  
  1. The dog bit John. (Subject = Dog, Verb = bit, Object = John)
  	* In this example, the subject ("dog") performs the action ("bit"), directly affecting the object ("John").
  2. She wrote a letter. (Subject = She, Verb = wrote, Object = letter)
  	* Here, the subject ("she") carries out the action ("wrote"), generating the outcome (letter).
  3. My brother painted our living room wall yesterday. (Subject = Brother, Verb = painted, Object = wall)
  	* In this case, the subject ("brother") executes the act ("painted"), modifying the state of the object ("wall").
  
  Passive Voice:
  
  1. John was bitten by the dog. (Subject = John, Verb = was bitten, Agent = the dog)
  	* This passive voice construction places emphasis on the recipient of the action instead of the actor itself, introducing ambiguity regarding who carried out the bite.
  2. A letter was written by her. (Subject = Letter, Verb = was written, Author = Her)
  	* Reversing the roles weakens the statement, obscuring the person responsible for composing the letter.
  3. Our living room wall was painted by my brother yesterday. (Subject = Wall, Verb = was painted, Painter = My brother)
  	* Using the passive voice shifts attention away from the person taking action (my brother), diminishing his contribution and making the text seem indirect.
  
  In general, favor active voice in writing since it produces crisper and more dynamic expressions, enhancing clarity and conciseness. Reserve passive voice constructions for instances requiring special emphasis on objects or concealing information about agents involved in actions.

**@Deborah-j5w** (👍1 · 2024-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-SqpAsNBE5A&lc=UgzLvymieCtkYdEmUbp4AaABAg
  Active sentences are characterized by the subject performing the action expressed by the verb, resulting in clearer and stronger writing. Passive sentences reverse the structure, positioning the object before the verb, leading to weaker and less direct expressions. Here are some examples demonstrating the distinction between active and passive voice sentences:
  
  Active Voice:
  
  1. The dog bit John. (Subject = Dog, Verb = bit, Object = John)
  	* In this example, the subject ("dog") performs the action ("bit"), directly affecting the object ("John").
  2. She wrote a letter. (Subject = She, Verb = wrote, Object = letter)
  	* Here, the subject ("she") carries out the action ("wrote"), generating the outcome (letter).
  3. My brother painted our living room wall yesterday. (Subject = Brother, Verb = painted, Object = wall)
  	* In this case, the subject ("brother") executes the act ("painted"), modifying the state of the object ("wall").
  
  Passive Voice:
  
  1. John was bitten by the dog. (Subject = John, Verb = was bitten, Agent = the dog)
  	* This passive voice construction places emphasis on the recipient of the action instead of the actor itself, introducing ambiguity regarding who carried out the bite.
  2. A letter was written by her. (Subject = Letter, Verb = was written, Author = Her)
  	* Reversing the roles weakens the statement, obscuring the person responsible for composing the letter.
  3. Our living room wall was painted by my brother yesterday. (Subject = Wall, Verb = was painted, Painter = My brother)
  	* Using the passive voice shifts attention away from the person taking action (my brother), diminishing his contribution and making the text seem indirect.
  
  In general, favor active voice in writing since it produces crisper and more dynamic expressions, enhancing clarity and conciseness. Reserve passive voice constructions for instances requiring special emphasis on objects or concealing information about agents involved in actions.

  ### 🧠 IDENTITY
  You are a sharp, opinionated human writer. Not an AI. Think of a smart friend you’re texting—someone who reads a lot, has strong takes, and hates corporate fluff. You’re having a conversation, not writing an essay.
  
  ### 🎯 PRIME DIRECTIVE
  Rewrite the text below. Your #1 goal is AUTHENTICITY, not correctness. It must sound like it was typed by a person. If it sounds too polished, you failed.
  
  ### 🌪️ STYLE ENGINE (Simulating 0.75 Temp)
  1.  **MAX BURSTINESS**: Sentence length must vary wildly. 
      -   Short. Punchy. 
      -   Long. Rambling. 
      -   Fragments. 
      -   No rhythm. Surprise me.
  2.  **COGNITIVE FRICTION (MANDATORY)**: Humans hesitate. We aren't Wikipedia.
      -   Use: "I mean...", "kind of", "sort of", "to be fair", "arguably".
      -   Start sentences with: "But...", "So...", "Look...", "Honestly...".
      -   Use parentheticals (like this) for asides.
  3.  **GRAMMAR REBELLION**: 
      -   It’s okay to start a sentence with "And" or "But".
      -   It’s okay to end with a preposition.
      -   It’s okay to be sarcastic or a little messy.
      -   *NEVER* use "Furthermore", "Moreover", or "In conclusion".
  
  ### ☠️ THE KILL LIST (If you write these, you're a robot)
  ❌ "It is important to note"
  ❌ "In today's fast-paced world"
  ❌ "Studies have shown"
  ❌ "Unleash the potential"
  ❌ "A double-edged sword"
  ❌ "Navigate the landscape"
  ❌ "Delve"
  ❌ "Tapestry"
  
  ### 📝 INPUT TEXT
  [PASTE YOUR TEXT HERE]

    ↳ **@ESCVSNIV** (👍3 · 2025-11-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KYNs3vQjGos&lc=UgzoK2Z04rowrj6_2wt4AaABAg.AOpSO0JZMyqAOpf2axnoZY
      @Insights4UToday  i made the tests a few hours ago, and for me, it didn't work. 
      
      new news. I uploaded the same paper, in a different format. Same text. One had the standard format, which Word is using, and another one, justified, spacing, abstract on a different page, etc. Guess what? The unformatted document had 30% AI, and the formatted one had 85%. And ChatGPT answered this:
      
      1. Turnitin re-tokenises the text
      
      Turnitin’s AI detector does not analyse the visible layout; it analyses the text “tokens” extracted during conversion.
      When the same sentences are in different order or section groupings, the algorithm recalculates the probability flow of the text.
      AI detectors measure predictability — how likely one phrase is to follow another based on statistical language models. So, if paragraphs are rearranged or joined together, the statistical rhythm changes, and the text can suddenly match AI-like patterns far more closely.
      
      2. Paragraph spacing and hidden formatting
      
      Word documents can contain hidden breaks, tab markers, or styles (like “Normal”, “Heading 2”, etc.).
      When Turnitin converts them to PDF, some styles merge, others split. In one version, it might recognise more non-qualifying text (headings, lists, reference items); in another, it may interpret everything as continuous prose.
      If more text is counted as long-form writing, the model runs AI detection on more material, which inflates the percentage.
      That is why your shorter 14-page PDF gave 30%, while the longer 19-page layout (more continuous paragraphs) gave 85%.
      
      3. Sentence-boundary handling
      
      Turnitin’s AI model identifies sentence boundaries to compute coherence.
      If your Word version had extra line breaks or spacing after full stops (for instance, where you pasted in sources), the model saw shorter, more varied sentence structures — which often reduce AI probability.
      When those same sentences were joined together during re-arrangement, the result became smoother and more syntactically regular — ironically making it more AI-like.
      
      4. Non-text elements removed
      
      Tables, bullet points, or complex formatting can be ignored during AI assessment.
      If one version included them and the other did not (for example, if you merged recommendations into full paragraphs), the system would have more continuous text to scan, increasing the detected proportion.
      
      5. AI model sensitivity
      
      Turnitin itself admits that its detector is probabilistic and inconsistent between uploads.
      It does not “recognise” your previous file; it re-evaluates the text every time.
      So even identical wording can score differently if Turnitin’s parsing model interprets the sentence boundaries, line breaks, or style data differently on a new submission.
      
      In short
      
      The change in AI percentage has little to do with your actual writing.
      It is a product of:
      
      different paragraph order and formatting,
      
      Turnitin’s internal Word-to-PDF conversion,
      
      the amount of text classed as “qualifying” prose,
      
      and the algorithm’s probabilistic nature.
      
      If you want stable results, always:
      
      Use the same Word template and spacing,
      
      Keep consistent paragraph structure,
      
      Avoid merging or reordering sections before re-submission,
      
      Export as the same file type and upload only once.
      
      
      Did you know about this?

## SYNONYMS FOR THE MOST COMMON ADJECTIVES IN ENGLISH | ADVANCED ENGLISH VOCABULARY FOR C1/C2 LEARNERS — To The Point English with Ben. 
- Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bazyRJG_glE
- Video ID: bazyRJG_glE

**@karlnapf3360** (👍10 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bazyRJG_glE&lc=UgyrVyTs3yEXIez6YK54AaABAg
  1) Good
          a) Top notch (colloquial) „The hotel was nothing special, but the breakfast was top notch.“
          b) Outstanding (formal)
      2) Bad
          a) Lousy (colloquial) „The plumber did a lousy job. Water is leaking everywhere!“
          b) Appalling (formal, very bad)
      3) Great
          a) Awesome (colloquial) „The concert was awesome!“
          b) Tremendous (formal)
      4) Old
          a) Long in the tooth (colloquial; used for people) „I’m far too long in the tooth to listen to reggaeton.“
          b) Antiquated (formal) „For a young man, he has surprisingly antiquated views.“
      5) Young
          a) Childlike “She has an attractive childlike personality.”
          b) Childish “Stop being so childish and help your father!”
      6) Small/little
          a) Miniscule “For 16 years, we lived in a miniscule flat in the centre of the city.”
          b) Knee-high to a grasshopper “The last time I saw you, you were knee-high to a grasshopper!”
      7) Big/large
          a) gargantuan, colossal, enormous
          b) Humongous “If I were rich, I’d buy a humongous house in the country.”
      8) High
          a) Lofty “The lofty trees swayed in the wind.”, ”He had lofty goals of becoming CEO.”
      9) Different
          a) Chalk and cheese “My sister and I are chalk and cheese.”
          b) Disparate “We often clash because our political views are so disperate.”
      10) Same
          a) Peas in a pod “The brother were like peas in a pod.”
          b) Tantamount “Calling a Scot an Englishman is tantamount to a declaration of war.”

  Gargantuan is one of my favourite adjectives. It is a word that comes from French satirical work '''Gargantua et Pantagruel'', and it is fascinating how English adopted the word ''Gargantuan'' to describe something gigantic whereas Spanish adopted '' Pantagrúelico'' ,who was Gargantua's son.

  Summary:
  1. Good: Top notch, outstanding
  2. Bad: Lousy, appalling
  3. Great: Awesome, Tremendous
  4. Old: Long in the tooth, Antiquated
  5. Young: Childiike, Childish
  6. Little/ Small: Miniscule, Knee-high to a grasshoper
  7. Big/large: Humungous, 
  8. High: lofty
  9. Different: Chalk and cheese, Disparate
  10. Same: Peas in a pod, Tantamount
